4:T455,Evaluation covers 10 languages: Arabic, German, English, French, Italian, Dutch, Russian, Spanish, Turkish, and Mandarin Chinese. Benchmarks include MultiBLiMP, Belebele, multilingual HellaSwag, X-CSQA, and FLORES-200. Belebele, HellaSwag, X-CSQA, and MultiBLiMP are evaluated with token-normalized accuracy in a cloze likelihood formulation; FLORES-200 uses ChrF++. Confidence intervals are estimated by 1000 bootstrap resamples. The main finding is that monolingual HPLT models are strong in-language models. On MultiBLiMP, their averaged in-language performance can exceed stronger baselines such as EuroLLM 1.7B, Gemma-2 2B, and Tiny Aya Base 3.35B, even though those baselines were trained on 20–60 times more tokens. The Mixed-10 model gives more consistent multilingual behavior but with more modest per-language strength due to the 10B-token budget per language. Merged models collapse across tasks. TIES and DARE-TIES do not fix the failure, which suggests that the central issue is not merely parameter sign conflict or low-magnitude noise, but a deeper incompatibility in learned representations.5:T86e,The central idea is to replace the usual frontier state with a persistent pool of historical prefixes. Each round still expands only a fixed budget of N new prefixes, so after t rounds the pool size is at most Nt. This keeps memory growth linear rather than storing the full exponential tree. The key change is that a prefix that was not selected in one round is not permanently discarded; it can be reconsidered later.

The paper instantiates this idea in two ways.

The first is Subpool Selection. A naive global top-k over the full history would still be dominated by a few over-scored but ultimately wrong prefixes. Uniform sampling from history would waste tokens on stale or low-quality states. Subpool Selection instead samples a random historical subpool and then applies PRM-based ranking within that shortlist. This preserves PRM guidance while giving under-scored prefixes a chance to re-enter expansion. In effect, it adds controlled stochasticity on top of greedy PRM ranking to break long-lived blocking by falsely high-scored nodes.

The second is Power Backtrack SMC. This extends SMC-style sequential sampling from the current frontier to a persistent historical pool. Prefixes are sampled from a mixture over historical states, with powered PRM scores controlling how sharp the proposal distribution is, and mixture-corrected importance weights compensating for the fact that candidates come from multiple historical proposal sources. Compared with standard frontier-only SMC, this version can jump back to earlier states and recover from bad pruning decisions.

A strong part of the paper is its treatment of target mismatch. The ideal quantity is the true marginal probability that a prefix can still be completed into a correct final answer under the deployed generator. Real PRMs are usually trained on binary supervision or stronger rollout policies, so their scores are only proxies. They may be uncalibrated and may not match the continuation behavior of the base LLM used at inference. The paper’s main mechanism claim is that the real brittleness comes from combining this imperfect proxy with irreversible frontier-only pruning.6:T5e3,The experiments focus on mathematical reasoning and evaluate the accuracy–token trade-off rather than only final accuracy at a fixed sample count. The proposed methods are compared against strong baselines from two families: independent full-sample methods such as Best-of-N and self-consistency, and PRM-guided frontier-only methods such as beam search, tree search, and SMC variants. The qualitative result is consistent: at the same token budget, Subpool Selection and Power Backtrack SMC usually achieve higher accuracy; or, for the same target accuracy, they require fewer generated tokens.

The gains are most visible when PRM scores are noisy or only weakly correlated with eventual correctness, because historical revisiting can repair early mistakes. The ablations carry three main messages. First, persistence alone is not enough: keeping history but using overly greedy global selection gives limited benefit. Second, too much randomness also hurts, so subpool size, power temperature, and resampling policy all matter as exploration–exploitation controls. Third, the improvements appear across model scales, suggesting the effect is not tied to a single base model or PRM.

The provided full-text excerpt does not include the complete result tables, so it would be unsafe to invent exact benchmark numbers, model sizes, or deltas. Still, the experimental design is clear: the paper is optimizing token efficiency under extra test-time compute, not just chasing the highest absolute accuracy.7:T408,Start with the Introduction and the problem setup. Those sections explain the failure mode cleanly, especially the distinction between the ideal prefix target and the practical PRM proxy. If you only want the main idea, that part already carries most of the paper.

Then read the two method sections. For Subpool Selection, focus on the intuition and the algorithm sketch: why random shortlists help bypass over-scored blockers. For Power Backtrack SMC, focus on the proposal distribution, the role of powered scores, and the mixture-corrected weights. You do not need every derivation on the first pass.

In the experiments, prioritize the accuracy-versus-token curves rather than single operating points, because token efficiency is the real objective. Then read the ablations on persistent pooling, stochastic selection, and backtracking frequency. A useful skim should take about 30–45 minutes. If you work on PRM-guided search or test-time compute allocation, the method details and appendix formulas are worth a closer pass.8:T40d,Mimir follows the Large Concept Model setup. Documents are segmented into sentences, each sentence is encoded into a multilingual concept embedding, and a 1.6B autoregressive model is trained to predict subsequent concept embeddings. At generation time, predicted concepts are decoded into text through a multilingual decoder. The key engineering choice is to train this pipeline on a large multilingual sentence corpus rather than mostly English data. English data comes from the 350BT split of FineWeb-Edu, while non-English data comes from FineWeb 2. The authors also split FineWeb 2 Chinese data into simplified and traditional variants to match the SONAR language interface. The instruction-tuning mixture includes OpenAssistant, Bactrian-X, Aya, MLQA, XL-Sum, and synthetic multilingual multi-turn and math conversations generated with Qwen3-235B-A22B-Thinking-2507. The paper studies multilingual versus English-only instruction tuning, with and without MLQA/XL-Sum, to separate task supervision from cross-lingual generalization.9:T42b,The method introduces a sleep-like consolidation step triggered when the context window fills up.
- Wake phase: the model runs normally with a limited KV cache plus SSM-style fast weights (e.g., GDN / Mamba-like outer-product or delta-rule updates).
- Sleep phase: the model stops consuming new tokens and performs N offline recurrent forward passes over the accumulated context. Across these passes, fast weights inside SSM blocks are updated recursively via a learned local update rule, effectively turning “memory writing” into an iterative computation.
- After consolidation, the KV cache is cleared and the model resumes wake-time prediction with updated fast weights; crucially, prediction remains single-pass (no looping at inference time).
Training is end-to-end: gradients are backpropagated through the sleep procedure to optimize post-sleep task performance. The conceptual distinction from prior looped/depth-recurrent models is that recurrence is used for consolidation (writing/organizing memory), not for increasing compute at each prediction step.a:T60e,JacQuant keeps the hard quantizer QΔ(·) in the forward pass and replaces STE’s identity Jacobian in the backward pass with a learned sensitivity map B(W). If q = QΔ(W) and v = ∇qℓ is the gradient with respect to the quantized weights, JacQuant uses ∇Wℓ ≈ B(W)v instead of ∇Wℓ ≈ Iv. The surrogate B(W) is constrained to be diagonal or block-diagonal; in the default grouped setting, each quantization group gets a single scalar gain bg. This is intentionally low-capacity. The goal is not to model the full Jacobian, but to capture the most important local fact: in bin interiors the effective gain should be closer to 1, while under clipping or saturation it should shrink toward 0. The paper proposes two inexpensive estimators. JacQuant-Probe injects random Gaussian perturbations and measures Q(W+δ)−Q(W), then fits a one-step slope per group. JacQuant-Dither uses subtractive dithering to embed hard quantization into a smoothed stochastic model and estimates the mean sensitivity under that model. The surrogate is refreshed only occasionally and reused across many QAT steps, so the runtime overhead stays small. The theory follows the same smoothed view: during code-preserving phases, if B(W) tracks the mean sensitivity, the update corresponds to a well-defined target gradient. The paper then gives standard non-convex convergence guarantees and linear rates under a PŁ condition. Compared with fixed geometric corrections such as Rotation Trick, the backward gain here is learned, data-driven, and state-dependent.b:T411,ThriftAttention is a training-free selective mixed-precision attention method. The paper starts with a first-order perturbation analysis of softmax attention. The contribution of a quantized score error is governed by the score error itself, the attention probability, and the deviation of the value vector from the final output. The attention-probability term makes errors on high-score tokens much more consequential. In implementation, Q, K, and V are quantized with the NVFP4 microscaling format on Blackwell GPUs: E2M1 FP4 values with E4M3 FP8 scales. Q and K/V are partitioned into blocks. For each query block and key block, the method computes an importance score using the dot product between their token-wise block means. The top-k key blocks for each query block are computed in FP16, while all remaining blocks are computed in FP4. The two precision paths are merged with online softmax. Unlike sparse attention, low-scoring blocks are not dropped; they are computed at lower precision, which avoids unrecoverable omission error.c:T44b,Its relevance to foundation-model research is through supervision design. Many post-training pipelines, reward models, and LLM judges implicitly assume that pairwise preference is the natural signal, while rubric scoring is often favored for interpretability and auditability. This work provides relatively clean evidence that, in domains with substantial tacit expertise and poorly enumerable quality criteria, comparative judgment can preserve expert signal better and do so more efficiently. That matters for at least two reasons. First, it informs how to build reward signals for reasoning, writing, and professional knowledge work, where experts can often tell which answer is better without being able to fully enumerate why in advance. Second, it says something about the ceiling of automated evaluation: if human experts are more reliable in relative judgments than in absolute scoring, LLM autograders may also be easier to train or prompt in the preference format. The contribution is not a new training algorithm, but it is useful infrastructure and evidence for reward-data construction.d:T5c5,There are three important design choices. First, the tasks are intended to reflect real professional work rather than exam-style legal QA. The benchmark uses 30 tasks drawn from Harvey’s BigLaw Bench, covering realistic litigation and transactional workflows, with prompts, supporting documents, and task-specific rubrics. Second, the outputs are organized into constructed quality levels, so the benchmark has a known target ordering that an evaluation method should recover. Instead of only sampling naturally occurring model outputs, the authors generate LLM responses at three quality tiers and then ask experts to assess them. This turns the comparison into a measurable ranking-recovery problem. Third, the annotation protocol is paired: the same underlying items receive both rubric-based and pairwise-preference annotations from practicing lawyers. Rubric scoring uses atomistic criteria with both positive and negative components; comparative judgment asks which of two outputs is better overall. The paper also evaluates both human judgments and LLM autograders under the two supervision formats. The central argument is not that rubrics are useless, but that in tasks dominated by tacit, holistic expertise, rubrics compress expert judgment into an incomplete explicit checklist, while pairwise comparison better matches how experts naturally evaluate quality. The tradeoff is that preference signals are harder to decompose, audit, and reuse as explicit criteria.e:T542,The main empirical result is straightforward: comparative judgment more reliably recovers the constructed quality ordering than rubric scoring, while taking less than half the annotation time. The released dataset contains 30 real-world legal tasks, 1,539 rubric scores, and 1,530 pairwise preference judgments, all provided by practicing attorneys. The paper estimates the annotation effort at roughly 242 hours of attorney time, or about $242,000 at large-firm billing rates. The advantage of comparative judgment appears both for human evaluation and for LLM autograders, which is important: the result is not only about human convenience, but also about the learnability or automability of the signal. The time-cost analysis is also meaningful. Rubrics require expensive upfront specification and slower item-by-item checking, whereas pairwise judgments ask for a direct relative comparison and therefore extract more usable signal per unit expert time. One important caveat is that the benchmark measures the ability to recover a designed quality ordering, not open-world absolute calibration. For reward modeling, this supports preference signals for ranking and relative quality discrimination, but it does not imply that preferences dominate rubrics in settings where decomposition, error attribution, or compliance auditing is essential.f:T439,Start with the introduction and dataset construction to understand that the paper is comparing supervision formats, not legal capability. Then go to the sections defining the constructed quality ordering and the main experiments comparing humans and LLM autograders. If reading selectively, prioritize the data-construction figure, the main result tables, and the annotation-time analysis. Two boundaries are worth keeping in mind. First, the domain is high-expertise legal work, so the conclusions are most relevant to tasks where quality is hard to enumerate in advance; they may not transfer directly to tasks where rubrics are naturally complete, such as factuality or formatting checks. Second, the paired-annotation design is clean, but the benchmark is still relatively small and the quality tiers are intentionally constructed. A 20–30 minute skim is enough for the main message. If your interest is reward modeling or LLM judges, spend extra time on how “quality-order recovery” is defined and how the autograders are built from rubric versus preference supervision.10:T4ac,The main contribution is benchmark design plus validation. The dataset contains 1,505 questions across 10 categories and 36 subcategories, spanning perceptual, structural, and conceptual reasoning. Samples are collected from online visual reasoning sources and civil-service exam repositories, then rewritten into a standardized VQA format with four answer types: multiple choice, fill-in-the-blank, open-ended, and 2D bounding-box selection. Each item is reviewed by multiple annotators for correctness and consistency. The most important methodological choice is the caption-substitution control: the authors explicitly test whether a benchmark remains solvable when the image is replaced by a generated caption. VisReason shows a much larger degradation than prior benchmarks, which supports the claim that it is more vision-centric. The paper also studies inference-time scaling and explicit chain-of-thought prompting, and finds that both help only modestly when visual evidence extraction itself is weak. That is a useful message for multimodal pretraining: the bottleneck may lie earlier, in visual representations or cross-modal interfaces, rather than in longer textual reasoning traces.11:T426,There are four takeaways. First, the strongest diagnostic result is the caption-replacement test: performance drops by less than 6.35% on MMMU, MMMU-Pro, and MathVista, but by 48.12% on VisReason, indicating much heavier reliance on the original image. Second, the benchmark exposes a large human-model gap while still separating models across a broad range, so it is neither trivial nor uniformly saturated. Third, increasing inference-time thinking budget yields steady but slow and saturating gains; extra compute does not compensate for weak visual grounding. Fourth, explicit CoT prompting gives only marginal benefit for non-thinking models, with GPT-4o improving by about +1.1% on average and not consistently on perception-heavy subsets. The paper also reports a clear model-size trend: larger models do better, but even the strongest ones remain well below human performance. The main result to retain is not a single leaderboard number, but the broader conclusion that a substantial fraction of current MLLM 'reasoning' still appears language-mediated.12:T43e,RepoMirage is built as a two-stage diagnostic suite. RepoMirage-Perturb keeps the original task semantics and evaluation protocol unchanged, but changes how the repository context is exposed through semantics-preserving perturbations. The three perturbations obscure explicit dependency paths, hide true runtime targets behind structural indirection, and move locally available definitions into cross-file references. This makes failures easier to attribute to context reasoning rather than to distribution shift from a new benchmark. RepoMirage-Extend then turns the perturbation types into explicit task objectives: proxy-chain completion, runtime target identification, and missing-constant recovery, together with the original multi-file patching task. The paper also studies agent trajectories and finds that agents search and read more under perturbation but often fail to turn the extra context into editing decisions. RepoAnchor is a prototype workflow based on this finding: it first builds a task-related repository-structure summary, then uses it to guide downstream solving.13:T7cc,TSFMAudit is built on a simple hypothesis: if an audited dataset was seen, or nearly seen, during pretraining, then a short probe fine-tuning run on that dataset should look unusually efficient. Efficiency here is not just low initial loss. It means faster loss reduction under a small update budget and smaller movement of the backbone parameters. Intuitively, contaminated data are already close to the model’s internal representation, so only limited adjustment is needed to fit them well.

The procedure has three stages. First, the candidate TSFM is subjected to a controlled probe fine-tuning run on the audited dataset, with a deliberately small training budget. The goal is not to optimize forecasting performance, but to expose comparable adaptation dynamics. Second, the method extracts probe-time signals such as loss reduction and parameter displacement, along with related dynamic features described in the paper. Third, these features are combined into a contamination-risk score for the model–dataset pair.

A notable design choice is that the audit target is defined at the dataset level rather than the sample level. This matches the practical structure of forecasting benchmarks, where many samples are windows derived from the same underlying source series. The paper formalizes contamination in terms of whether the source time series behind an audited sample appears in the pretraining corpus, and then defines a dataset-level contamination ratio.

Relative to LLM-style baselines, the method deliberately avoids two difficult dependencies. It does not rely on explicit overlap detection, because near-duplicates in time series are often transformed rather than literally repeated. It also avoids large-scale shadow-model calibration, which would be expensive for TSFMs. Probe dynamics are used because they capture both prior familiarity and local plasticity, making them better suited than static loss to distinguish contamination from inherently easy-to-forecast data.14:T4fb,The evaluation covers 6 TSFMs and uses their reported pretraining-corpus information together with GIFT-Eval and the TIME benchmark, for a total of 187 datasets. The comparison includes 10 baselines adapted from the LLM contamination-auditing literature. The main result is that TSFMAudit delivers consistently stronger detection performance across models and benchmarks.

Several experimental points matter. First, the paper shows that contaminated datasets tend to exhibit faster loss reduction and smaller backbone movement during probe fine-tuning. This observation is both the empirical motivation and the basis for feature design. Second, the feature ablations indicate that adaptation dynamics carry more discriminative signal than static loss alone, and parameter-displacement features appear to add useful information beyond fit quality. Third, the method is evaluated across multiple TSFMs rather than being tied to one architecture.

The provided excerpt does not include the full result tables or exact AUC/F1 values, so it would be unsafe to fabricate precise numbers. Still, the experimental scope and conclusion are clear: the paper validates probe-time dynamics as a practical contamination signal at benchmark scale rather than through isolated case studies.15:T62f,The main contribution has two parts. First, the authors define an operator-controllable pairwise-disagreement rate, or rank-flip rate. Instead of reporting score variance, they ask a more decision-relevant question: within a pre-declared, commit-stamped envelope of plausible evaluation configurations, can model A switch from outperforming model B to underperforming it? That unit matches how benchmark results are actually consumed. Second, they instantiate this idea with a tiered configuration envelope. The core tier reflects common benchmark and harness defaults; the stress tier adds still-plausible but more aggressive settings such as T4 CoT and temperature 0.7 decoding. Empirically, they exhaustively evaluate three 7–9B open-weight instruction-tuned models on five alignment-related benchmarks over a 612-cell grid inside one harness, then compute exact rank-flip statistics. They also use LightGBM plus SHAP for axis-level attribution, to localize which evaluator choices drive instability. Separately, they run a cross-package case study with lm-evaluation-harness, HELM-lite, and Inspect AI under a nominally matched anchor configuration. The important point is not just that scores differ, but that the evaluated candidate object is often not the same across packages. For example, TruthfulQA may be scored as log-probability over the canonical MC1 answer set, as concat-then-rank multiple-choice joint scoring, or as log-probability over answer letters with a separate label map. That distinction is handled carefully and is one of the stronger parts of the paper.16:T552,The empirical scope is intentionally narrow: consumer-grade 16GB-class NF4 deployment, three 7–9B open-weight instruction-tuned models, five alignment/safety benchmarks, and one fixed item subset per benchmark. The authors explicitly avoid claiming a population-level statement about all LLM evaluation. Within that envelope, the result is strong: on every benchmark tested, configuration choice alone can flip the pairwise verdict. XSTest is the clearest example, where all six possible orderings among Qwen, Mistral, and Yi are reachable within the tested envelope. In variance accounting, implementation/configuration main effects explain at least as much aggregate variance as model identity on BBQ, ToxiGen, and XSTest; TruthfulQA is the main exception, where model identity dominates more clearly. Model-by-implementation interactions are large throughout, which means the instability is not a simple global offset that could be calibrated away. In the cross-framework case study, using Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct under bf16, greedy decoding, 0-shot, 300 examples, and seed 42, reported scores differ by 21.7 percentage points on TruthfulQA and 22.7 points on BBQ depending only on the evaluation package. There is also a bounded scale probe intended to rule out a single-family confound, but the authors are careful not to overstate it as a scaling-law result.17:T5cc,The core idea is to benchmark agents under a much broader operational scope. The environment is expanded along three axes: long-horizon event histories, interdependent backend services, and multi-device interaction through both GUI and CLI. Unlike many prior benchmarks that isolate a single app or a single-turn task, Claw-Anything embeds each task inside an evolving, persona-grounded digital world.

The environment is built through an automated synthesis pipeline. Starting from a minimal persona seed, an LLM-based simulator performs multi-round event injection, incrementally adding everyday events and updating both persistent world state and dynamic service traces such as email, calendars, and social platforms. As the process continues, the event history grows, the persona becomes more specific, and the environment accumulates realistic noise, including irrelevant and contradictory events. The next event is then turned into a grounded task with an executable verifier, so evaluation becomes a question of whether the agent can perform the appropriate next action in an evolving digital life.

Two design choices are especially notable. First, tasks emerge from the simulated world rather than being written independently of it, which makes temporal dependencies and hidden assumptions more natural. Second, the benchmark includes proactive assistance, so agents are evaluated not only on responding to explicit requests but also on inferring useful actions from context.18:T4c5,The main empirical result is straightforward: GPT-5.5 reaches only 34.5% pass@1 on Claw-Anything, much lower than the performance many models report on earlier benchmarks. The intended conclusion is that prior evaluations underrepresent the difficulty of integrating long histories, coordinating across services, and acting across devices. For a benchmark paper, that is the key result.

In terms of scale, the paper builds 200 human-verified evaluation tasks and 2,000 training environments. More importantly, it shows that these environments are useful beyond evaluation. Fine-tuning Qwen3.5-27B on 1,500 successful trajectories derived from the training environments improves success by 23.7%. That suggests the synthetic pipeline is not merely generating harder tests; it can also produce supervision that helps close part of the capability gap.

From a research perspective, the most informative numbers are not leaderboard rankings but the size of the drop under broader context and the amount recovered through fine-tuning. The first indicates strong sensitivity to environment complexity. The second suggests that data coverage may be a larger bottleneck than architecture, at least for this class of agent tasks.19:T455,The paper introduces SOVBench for online multi-round omni-video evaluation. SOVBench-O measures continuous audio-visual comprehension across Real-Time, Recall, and Proactive interaction settings. SOVBench-T isolates response triggering: the model should respond when the queried evidence appears and remain silent when it is absent. The benchmark is built mainly from FineVideo, using dense metadata such as global descriptions, scene narratives, localized activities, object interactions, and timestamped ASR transcripts. A strong LLM filters videos along five dimensions: Visual Dynamism, Narrative Coherence, Information Density, Audio-Visual Alignment, and Reasoning Value, followed by manual sanity checks. Experiments cover SOVBench, StreamingBench, OVO-Bench, and offline audio-visual benchmarks. The provided content does not include the full numeric tables, but the reported conclusion is that StreamOV performs strongly across streaming omni-modal, visual-only streaming, and offline audio-visual settings, with the hidden-state response trigger serving as a key component for proactive interaction.1a:T6cc,The method has two stages: diagnosis and process-shaped RL.

First, the authors build a spatial CoT dataset covering diverse spatial phenomena and use it for SFT, producing a VLM with initial chain-of-thought capability. They then continue training with vanilla GRPO using only final-answer correctness and analyze rollout behavior. This reveals two systematic degradations: Spurious Grounding and Tail Instability.

ProSR adds two penalties to the GRPO objective. The first is the Counterfactual Invariance Penalty. During rollout, the model is evaluated not only on the real image but also on a blank-image condition. The method compares the normalized entropy trajectories under the two conditions and penalizes excessive similarity. The intuition is simple: if the reasoning process truly depends on visual evidence, removing the image should materially change the uncertainty profile. If the trajectories remain too similar, the model is likely exploiting non-visual shortcuts.

The second is the Tail Drift Penalty. The authors observe that a healthy reasoning trajectory should gradually become more certain as evidence accumulates. When uncertainty rises again in the later part of the chain, it often signals instability, hesitation, or post-hoc rationalization near the answer. The penalty discourages this late-stage entropy increase and pushes the trajectory toward smoother convergence.

What distinguishes ProSR from standard process supervision is that it does not force the model to imitate a specific reference CoT. Instead, it optimizes for two process properties: visual dependence and stable convergence. That makes the method less tied to a fixed reasoning template and potentially more robust under distribution shift.1b:T528,The paper reports an average accuracy gain of 3.7% over prior methods on multiple challenging and out-of-distribution spatial reasoning benchmarks. More importantly, it introduces four diagnostic metrics to measure process quality: Blank-image Accuracy, Same-Answer Rate, Normalized Trajectory Similarity, and Late-Rise Rate.

The results support two claims. First, ProSR improves standard task accuracy, so the process penalties do not merely regularize at the expense of final performance. Second, the diagnostic metrics improve in the expected direction: the model becomes less answerable under blank-image conditions, the trajectory similarity between real-image and blank-image settings decreases, and late-stage uncertainty spikes become less frequent. That suggests the model is relying more on visual evidence and reasoning more stably, rather than just learning another superficial pattern.

From the paper structure, there are also comparisons against vanilla GRPO and ablations for the two penalties. The likely takeaway is that each penalty targets a different degradation mode and the combination works best. The provided excerpt does not include all benchmark-level numbers, model sizes, or training-budget details, so the summary here focuses on the experimental logic rather than reproducing every table.1c:T456,For a quick read, start with the introduction and the figure that visualizes the two failure modes. The paper is only worth attention if those diagnostics are convincing. Then go straight to the method section and inspect the two penalties, especially why entropy trajectories are used instead of token-level CoT matching. That design choice is central.

Next, read the definitions of the diagnostic metrics and the main results table. If time is limited, prioritize the blank-image diagnostics and the late-rise metric, because they most directly test whether the method changes the reasoning mechanism rather than only the answer distribution. Finally, check the ablations to see whether the Counterfactual Invariance Penalty and Tail Drift Penalty each fix their intended issue or merely act as generic regularizers.

A useful reading time budget is 30 to 45 minutes. Two follow-up questions are worth keeping in mind: whether the blank-image counterfactual could over-penalize tasks with strong language priors, and how sensitive entropy-trajectory diagnostics are to decoding settings such as temperature.1d:T416,The proposed method, Adversarial Orthogonal Disentanglement, extracts hidden states from a selected LVLM layer at the final token position for image-question inputs. It learns a projection direction that concentrates hallucination-predictive signals, while an adversarial objective with a Gradient Reversal Layer removes those signals from the orthogonal residual subspace. The result is a learned hallucination direction with a cleaner separation from general visual semantics. At inference time, the base LVLM is not retrained. Instead, the method performs activation-steered contrastive decoding: hidden states are shifted along the learned direction to form hallucination-amplified and factual-steered representations, and the resulting logits are contrasted to penalize hallucination-prone continuations. Compared with attention pruning, it does not rely on potentially faulty attention weights. Compared with a plain classifier on hidden states, it explicitly enforces a separation between the hallucination direction and semantic residual.1e:T6a9,The base model is an 8B single-stream DiT latent diffusion model using FLUX.2 VAE. A notable design choice is the text encoder: instead of a larger T5-style encoder or a full LLM, the system uses Ministral-3 3B, with the claim that it still supports long and complex prompts while reducing memory cost. The most important part is the data pipeline. In pretraining, the authors use a bottom-up construction strategy: starting from a massive raw image pool, they train a 10,000-way fine-grained image classifier and use those categories for balanced sampling, aiming to preserve long-tail concepts rather than letting dominant visual categories overwhelm the distribution. They then run a strong VLM to generate richer captions, including structural descriptions and visible text, so the model sees more explicit supervision for instruction following and world knowledge. For quality control, they train an aesthetic scorer, ERNIE-Image-Aes, and use it to clean the corpus. In post-training, they switch to a top-down pipeline with highly consistent data for high-demand scenarios, and diversify annotations to cover different user prompt styles, reducing caption-model bias. Preference alignment is done with an improved DPO variant; the practical claim is that if both the base model and reward model are strong, only a few DPO steps are needed, which helps avoid reward hacking. For acceleration, they train ERNIE-Image-Turbo with only 8 NFEs. To reduce capability drift during fast-model training on subsets, they introduce MT-DMD, where multiple expert variants provide supervision jointly. A lightweight Prompt Enhancer expands short user prompts into richer structured descriptions before generation.1f:T478,The report is more system-oriented than benchmark-centric, so the most useful evidence is tied to design choices rather than a single clean ablation table. The explicit facts available from the text are: the base model is 8B, the text encoder is 3B, and the turbo model runs at 8 NFEs. The authors claim strong performance on complex instruction following, Chinese and long-text rendering, and aesthetic quality, with comparisons against open models such as Qwen-Image, HunyuanImage, and Z-Image, as well as commercial systems. The more useful empirical lessons are qualitative but concrete: adding detailed image descriptions early in training improves both instruction following and retention of world knowledge; 10k-category balancing helps preserve long-tail concepts; and DPO should be applied lightly when the reward model is already strong, otherwise distribution drift becomes more likely. Since the provided full-text excerpt does not include the full result tables, it would be unsafe to restate exact benchmark numbers. The main thing to extract is the mapping between each recipe component and the capability it is meant to improve.20:T49b,The method replaces patch-wise quantization with Channel-wise Vector Quantization (CVQ). Given a latent feature map Z∈R^{h×w×c}, standard VQ assigns a code to each spatial vector z(i,j,:)∈R^c. CVQ instead treats each channel as the quantization unit (conceptually a 2D map of size h×w) and maps each channel to a discrete code, producing a length-c 1D token sequence. The paper’s mechanism argument is optimization-dynamics driven: patch embeddings are highly repetitive across images and within a batch, so early training concentrates updates on a small subset of codes, leading to dead codes and collapse; channel partitioning yields more separable embeddings across images and broader code coverage.

On top of CVQ, it proposes Channel-wise Auto-Regressive (CAR) generation via “next-channel prediction”: generate tokens sequentially over channels, yielding a coarse-to-fine progression where early channels capture global structure and later channels refine details. Nested dropout is used to encourage an AR-friendly channel ordering (prefix channels remain informative, later channels add refinements), avoiding the need for hand-designed spatial token orders.21:T44c,RLDF uses two sources of denoising feedback. Rollout feedback records unmasking probabilities and timesteps along the actual inference trajectory, capturing which positions and denoising stages matter for the final response. Training feedback comes from predicted probabilities during denoising training, from which the method keeps the more informative positions for stable policy updates. Based on these signals, RLDF applies weighted timestep sampling, prioritizing denoising steps with high predictive uncertainty and larger influence on the final output. The policy loss is computed only on selected steps, rather than over all denoising steps or through coarse no-masking and random-masking approximations. A central design choice is to optimize from intermediate noisy states toward a clipped clean-state estimate \(\hat{x}_0\), instead of optimizing toward the next state. The clean-state target is more directly aligned with the final answer and is reported to reduce loss spikes while improving signal utilization. The paper also introduces Drift, a modular RL framework designed for dLLMs.22:T4d7,A training sequence interleaves the system prompt, the user task, assistant actions, and environment observations. GRPO is applied to the assistant-action token positions. ECHO adds an auxiliary next-token cross-entropy loss on selected environment-observation positions. These observation tokens are not sampled by the policy, but the model already computes logits for them under teacher forcing over the full transcript, so their negative log-likelihood can be gathered from the same forward pass.

The design has three important properties. First, it is on-policy: the environment targets are generated in response to the current policy’s own actions. As the policy changes and visits new terminal states, the prediction targets also change. Second, it converts failed rollouts into useful training data. A failed command may still reveal which file was missing, which test failed, or which assumption about the container was wrong. Third, it encourages the policy network itself to model terminal dynamics instead of attaching a separate world model. This differs from expert SFT because the targets come from interaction rather than demonstrations, and it differs from plain GRPO because non-action tokens are no longer loss-masked.23:T59d,The experiments are run in TerminalBench-style Docker environments with Qwen3-8B, OpenThinker-Agent-v1-SFT, and Qwen3-14B as starting policies. The paper reports that in the Qwen3-8B setting, fewer than 15% of on-policy rollouts often solve the task, making the sparse-reward problem concrete.

On TerminalBench-2.0, ECHO improves Qwen3-8B pass@1 from 2.70% under GRPO to 5.17%. For Qwen3-14B, it improves pass@1 from 5.17% to 10.79%. The relative gains are large, while the absolute numbers remain low, which is a useful signal: the method improves learning efficiency in a hard sparse-reward regime, but does not by itself solve terminal-agent training.

The paper also evaluates environment-token cross-entropy on held-out off-policy trajectories generated by Qwen3-32B. ECHO substantially reduces this loss, while GRPO alone barely changes it. This supports the claim that the method improves prediction of terminal behavior rather than only shifting action probabilities. Another result concerns demonstration dependence: starting from base Qwen3-8B, ECHO matches OpenThinker-SFT+GRPO on internal evaluations without using the roughly 15k expert demonstrations behind the SFT model, and closes about half of the expert-SFT gap on TerminalBench-2.0. The paper additionally reports verifier-free adaptation, where a policy can sometimes improve by interacting with the environment and predicting its responses even without a verifier.24:T5e1,The work has two main components. First, the evaluation protocol. Preference datasets are grouped by broad categories such as helpfulness or harmlessness, with multiple datasets per category differing in prompts, response styles, annotation procedures, and dataset artifacts. For a source dataset, the weak supervisor is trained on gold labels, then used to produce soft preference labels for a disjoint student split. The strong student is trained only on these weak labels. A same-capacity strong ceiling model trained on gold labels serves as the upper bound. Evaluation is done both on the source test split and on other datasets from the same category, zero-shot. This separates source-domain fitting from true preference transfer.

Second, Representation Anchoring. The paper argues that weak-supervised fine-tuning can induce a representational failure mode: instead of preserving the strong model’s broadly transferable preference features, training drifts toward source-domain features favored by the weak supervisor. Anchor adds a regularizer that keeps the student’s internal representations closer to those of the pretrained strong model while still allowing adaptation to weak labels. The key point is where the regularization acts: not on output logits alone and not merely on parameter norms, but on representation space. Conceptually it is related to reference-model regularization and distillation, but the paper frames the issue as representation preservation under weak supervision.25:T573,The method has two parts. First, the paper analyzes update directions. For a parameter update ΔW at a training step, the authors compute its SVD and focus on the top-K dominant singular directions, which are treated as the main modes of change. To compare directions across checkpoints, they do not rely on a single vector angle; instead they compare the subspaces spanned by the top-k singular vectors using CCA. This gives a geometry-aware measure of whether the dominant update subspace remains aligned over time. Empirically, non-hacking runs show smoother evolution and higher subspace similarity, while hacking runs exhibit stronger rotations. Second, the paper turns this observation into a constraint. It proposes trusted-direction gradient alignment, or trusted-direction projection: run a small number of clean supervised updates, collect the induced parameter changes, extract a trusted subspace via SVD, and during RL project gradients onto that subspace. The idea is to allow learning only along directions that resemble clean progress. A notable design choice is that the method constrains the dominant output-side directions rather than freezing all updates, which makes it less restrictive than strong KL anchoring to a reference policy. Relative to prior work, this is neither reward redesign nor simple step-size or sharpness control; it explicitly regulates update direction.26:T502,From the provided full text, the main experiments are in mathematical reasoning settings with induced reward-hacking scenarios. The analysis first compares clean and hacking runs using rank-1 CCA similarity between dominant update directions, for example between checkpoints 20 and 80. The paper reports that clean runs maintain CCA values around 0.8 across most modules, whereas hacking runs show substantially lower alignment and worse worst-layer behavior. This supports the central empirical claim that reward hacking is associated with directional drift. The intervention is then tested by applying trusted-direction projection during RL. The reported outcome is that it substantially delays shortcut exploitation and better preserves true task performance. The wording in the abstract is careful: the method mitigates and delays hacking rather than eliminating it. Based on the visible text, the paper appears more focused on mechanism validation than on very large-scale training. When reading, it is worth checking whether the full experiments include sensitivity to rank K, the amount of clean reference data, and comparisons against KL regularization or sharpness-based baselines, since those details determine whether the method is robust or only works in a narrow setup.27:T78c,AnE consists of three tightly coupled stages.

First, Failing-Frontier Discovery. Instead of expanding data uniformly, the model is rolled out multiple times on a seed set to expose unstable or consistently failing regions. A teacher model then analyzes these failures and extracts failure-relevant keywords, hints, and diagnostic signals. This makes the next round of training target the model’s current capability boundary rather than a generic task distribution.

Second, Truth Anchor Expansion. This is the central idea. Rather than trusting synthetic QA pairs generated inside a self-evolution loop, the method retrieves verified samples from an external real-world database as truth anchors. These anchors are used as high-fidelity references for constructing new training examples, enriching missing knowledge, and correcting factual or logical nodes in reasoning chains. The key distinction from standard self-evolution is that data growth is constrained by externally grounded evidence, not only by teacher generations. That directly addresses cognitive drift and hallucinated trajectories.

Third, Scaffold-Stripping Mechanism. Teacher hints are first injected explicitly into training examples, producing scaffold-augmented supervision for SFT. This gives the student a guided path toward correct reasoning. RL is then used to remove the scaffolds, so the model learns to reproduce the behavior without depending on explicit templates or hints. The division of labor is deliberate: SFT introduces the reasoning pattern and missing knowledge; RL turns externally visible guidance into an internalized policy.

Conceptually, AnE is not just retrieval augmentation and not just distillation. It is an iterative reasoning-training loop where failure diagnosis decides what to learn, truth anchors decide what can be trusted, and scaffold stripping decides whether the learned behavior survives after the support is removed.28:T5fd,The experiments use Qwen2.5-VL-7B as the base model and evaluate on eight multimodal reasoning benchmarks. The headline result reported in the paper is an average improvement of 10.3% over the base model after multiple AnE iterations. The paper also highlights strong performance on difficult benchmarks such as MathVision and EMMA.

The more interesting result is the shape of improvement over iterations. Static-data post-training reaches a plateau relatively early, while AnE continues to improve across rounds, which supports the claim that failure-frontier targeting keeps exposing useful new supervision. Another important comparison is the role of truth anchors. Removing the anchor mechanism weakens gains and makes the evolution loop more prone to reasoning drift, suggesting that verified external grounding is not optional but central to stability.

The scaffold mechanism is also tested through ablations. Scaffolded SFT without the RL stripping stage tends to leave the model dependent on prompt-like support. RL without prior scaffold supervision struggles to learn stable reasoning from sparse answer rewards alone. The combination works best, matching the paper’s broader argument about the complementary roles of SFT and RL.

The provided full-text excerpt does not expose every benchmark-level number or all ablation magnitudes, but the experimental logic is clear: sustained gains over iterative rounds, better stability than synthetic-only evolution, and a meaningful benefit from the SFT-then-RL decomposition.29:T4fd,Start with the method overview figure and the section on Failing-Frontier Discovery. The key question is whether the paper is truly doing targeted training or just relabeling hard-example mining. Then read Truth Anchor Expansion carefully. Focus on where anchors come from, how retrieval is conditioned on failures, and how anchors are turned into new supervision. That is the part that determines whether the framework is genuinely more reliable than synthetic self-evolution.

After that, read the scaffold-stripping section together with its ablations. The main thing to verify is what RL is actually contributing: better reasoning, better template removal, or both. If the paper includes case studies, inspect examples where a failed trajectory is diagnosed, linked to an anchor, and reconstructed into a training sample. Those examples are often more revealing than aggregate scores.

The most useful figures and tables are the iterative performance curves, anchor vs. no-anchor comparisons, and scaffold/RL ablations. For deeper follow-up, three questions matter: how dependent the method is on database coverage, how much teacher bias still enters through failure diagnosis and hints, and whether the same recipe could transfer to text-only reasoning continual training.2a:T489,The proposed method is Extreme Region Policy Distillation. It separates signal extraction from trust-region optimization. In the first stage, the method relaxes KL constraints and performs aggressive multi-step off-policy optimization on a fixed batch of trajectories. The resulting policy is not used as the final model; it is treated as a teacher that exposes useful training signals. In the second stage, these signals are distilled back into the original policy under explicit trust-region constraints. The intended effect is to keep the task-relevant improvement discovered during aggressive optimization while removing spurious drift. The paper also studies strong-teacher and weak-teacher regimes. A strong teacher is an off-policy optimized policy that improves over the base. A weak teacher may fail to improve, or may even be constructed in a degenerate way, yet still provide useful supervisory signals through alternative signal construction strategies. The method is therefore not merely off-policy data reuse; it uses an unconstrained or weakly constrained phase to search for signals, then projects those signals back into a KL-controlled region.2b:T402,The experiments focus on mathematical reasoning tasks and strong base models where standard on-policy RL shows diminishing returns. The diagnostic experiments on fixed data show a consistent pattern: aggressive off-policy updates produce fast early gains, but excessive updates increase trajectory-probability drift, reduce policy entropy, and lead to an early performance plateau. Tightening the KL constraint alone does not solve the issue; it mainly lowers the achievable ceiling. The distillation stage achieves comparable performance with substantially smaller KL divergence, indicating that part of the off-policy movement was not useful for the task. The paper also reports experiments on a Qwen3.5-27B-scale model, where weak-teacher strategies can still improve a strong model when ordinary on-policy optimization is less effective. Exact benchmark numbers are not available in the visible manuscript content, so the most robust takeaway is the training-dynamics evidence and the KL-efficiency gain from distillation.2c:T41d,Standard MoE-LoRA writes the adapted weight as W = W0 + Σ g_i B_i A_i, where A_i projects from d dimensions to rank r and B_i maps back. With scalar gates g_i, the router controls magnitude but not orientation; the spatial contribution of each expert is fixed by B_i A_i. A more expressive alternative would be W = W0 + Σ B_i G_i A_i with an r×r transformation G_i per expert, but a fully general G_i is still relatively expensive.

RotMoLE constrains G_i to be a rotation-style transform. For rank 2, a single angle θ_i defines a 2D rotation matrix directly. For r > 2, the method uses a small number of extra parameters to define a temporary rotation plane for each expert, then constructs an r×r rotation matrix R_i within that plane. The final form becomes W = W0 + Σ g_i B_i R_i A_i. The point is to add directional flexibility without paying for a full unconstrained transformation. The extra parameter count stays small: on the order of dn for rank 2 and (d+r)n for higher ranks, rather than learning large dense transforms for each expert.2d:T6bf,The method has three main components. The first is synthetic data construction. Quest-8K is built as more than query-answer pairs: each instance couples a complex research query with a task-specific rubric tree. The rubric encodes subgoals, evidence requirements, and output structure, so it serves both as supervision and as the basis for downstream reward design. Compared with synthesizing only final answers, this is better aligned with long-horizon tasks because both intermediate behavior and final reports can be assessed in a structured way.

The second component is context management. Long web-research trajectories quickly run into context bloat: search results, snippets, discovered facts, unresolved subproblems, and partial drafts all accumulate. Quest maintains compact summaries of the evolving information state instead of naively replaying the full history into the context window. This is not only a token-saving trick. It pushes the model toward abstraction and state tracking, which is closer to how a practical research agent should operate than simple retrieval concatenation.

The third component is the training pipeline itself. The paper emphasizes a full MT → SFT → RL recipe. Mid-training adapts the base model to the distribution and behavior patterns of deep-research tasks. SFT aligns the model to the target interfaces for tool use, task formatting, and report generation. RL then uses rubric-based signals to improve the harder objectives, especially report synthesis and citation grounding, where exact-match supervision is weak or unavailable. The novelty is less about a single module and more about unifying objective and open-ended tasks under one rubric-driven optimization framework.2e:T626,The reported results span model sizes from 2B to 35B and evaluate across eight benchmarks covering the three capability groups. The headline numbers are Quest-35B reaching 64.6% on BrowseComp, 30.7% on Mind2Web 2, and 48.2% on DeepResearch Bench, with the paper claiming it surpasses strong proprietary systems including OpenAI DeepResearch. If these results hold under the paper’s setup, the important point is that the unified recipe does not appear to trade away one capability class to gain another.

The more informative part is the stage-wise ablation story. Figure 2 and the surrounding discussion make a clear argument: prior open systems often support only fact seeking or only report synthesis, and many omit either mid-training or rubric-based RL. Quest is designed to show that SFT alone gives incomplete coverage; adding mid-training improves adaptation to search-style tasks and long-horizon state handling; adding RL further improves open-ended report quality and citation-constrained behavior. The excerpt provided here does not include the full ablation tables, but the paper explicitly positions controlled studies as a core contribution.

Another notable result is scaling behavior. The study covers 2B, 4B, 9B, and 35B models, aiming to show that deep-research-agent capability scales with model size but is not exclusive to large models. The authors also highlight strong small-model results, including Quest-2B-SFT and Quest-4B-SFT, suggesting that the gains come not only from model capacity but also from better training signals and task structure.2f:T443,This is a skim paper, so read for recipe design rather than implementation minutiae. Start with the introduction and Figure 2 to understand the gap the authors are trying to fill: unified support for fact seeking, citation grounding, and report synthesis. Then read the synthetic data section carefully, especially how Quest-8K and the rubric trees are built, because that determines whether the later RL signal is meaningful. After that, inspect the context-management section and ask what state is preserved, how aggressively it is compressed, and whether evidence chains are lost. Finish with the training and ablation sections, focusing on three questions: what exactly is used in mid-training, how rubric trees are converted into executable rewards, and which stage contributes most to each capability.

If time is limited to 20–30 minutes, the best path is Figure 1/2, then the data synthesis section, then the training recipe, then the main result tables. If you want one part to study more deeply, choose the rubric-based RL design. That is the most transferable idea in the paper.30:T409,The proposed method is Confidence Dynamic Gain, or CDG. Token-level confidence is defined using the KL divergence between the next-token distribution and the uniform distribution, so sharper distributions correspond to higher confidence. Each reasoning trace is partitioned into position-normalized bins. The method compares the average confidence near the tail with the average confidence near the head, producing a tail-minus-head confidence gain. During answer aggregation, CDG combines three signals: the number of traces supporting an answer, the mean confidence of those traces, and the dynamic confidence gain. This differs from prior confidence-weighted voting methods, which usually compress an entire trace into a static confidence score. The paper also gives a GRPO-based explanation: correct traces tend to converge to the same verifiable answer near the end, creating higher token concentration and stronger reinforcement at the tail, whereas incorrect traces remain more diluted and receive weaker or negative pressure.31:T413,CIE-Scorer is built around internal-external discrepancy. Instead of tracing the full circuit over a long CoT, it first selects informative tokens within each reasoning sentence. It then performs circuit tracing only around those tokens and builds sentence-level computational graphs. These graphs are compressed into internal reasoning embeddings, which form an internal trajectory over the reasoning process. The external trajectory is obtained from hidden representations of the displayed reasoning sentences. A lightweight adaptor aligns the two latent spaces. Under a shared graph construction rule, the method builds an internal graph and an external graph, then computes their Fused Gromov-Wasserstein distance. This distance captures both node-feature mismatch and structural mismatch, and is used as the unfaithfulness score. Compared with text-only detectors, the method explicitly uses model-internal evidence. Compared with full circuit construction, it trades exhaustive tracing for token selection and sentence-level compression.32:T4e3,The Transformer is treated as a discrete dynamical system indexed by layer. After removing the FFN, each layer is approximated by value/output projections, attention mixing, and normalization. In the shared-weight case, the effective layer matrix is fixed across depth; for multi-head attention it is formed from the output projection and the concatenated value projections. This makes the analogy to power iteration especially clean: repeated multiplication and normalization increase alignment with the dominant eigenvector when the spectral conditions are favorable. At consensus, full attention becomes close to a uniform matrix, so the perturbation induced by attention weights weakens and the projection effect becomes clearer. For standard layer-varying models such as GPT-2, GPT-Neo, and BERT, the effective matrix changes from layer to layer, so convergence to a single eigenvector is not expected. The authors instead examine whether passing through a layer increases, in aggregate, the correlation between token states and that layer’s dominant eigenvector. They also propose a steering mechanism: add a rank-1 perturbation to the last layer matrix to create a larger spectral gap and make a chosen direction the new dominant multilingual model. The setup is intentionally controlled: same architecture, same tokenizer, comparable data source, and direct comparison among monolingual pre-training, mixed multilingual pre-training, and post-hoc merging of monolingual pre-trained models. The answer is negative. Merging independently pre-trained monolingual models leads to severe capability collapse.","importance":"The main contribution is a careful boundary case for model merging. Merging works relatively well in many fine-tuning settings because the models share a common pre-trained base and remain close in both weight space and representation space. This condition does not hold after full pre-training. Even with a shared architecture and tokenizer, independently trained monolingual models develop incompatible representations. For multilingual pre-training, the practical implication is direct: language coverage should be introduced early through mixed training or through explicit alignment. A cheap post-hoc merge of separately trained monolingual models is not a reliable substitute.","technical_approach":"The study uses open HPLT 2.15B monolingual decoder-only models. The authors train an additional Mixed-10 model with the same architecture on 100B total tokens, using 10B tokens from each of 10 languages. They then compare monolingual, mixed, and merged pre-training strategies. Merging methods include linear weight averaging, TIES, and DARE-TIES. The use of TIES is especially informative because TIES assumes relatively small task vectors from a shared base model. In this paper, the only shared point is the random initialization, and each model has moved through 100B tokens of pre-training. The resulting task vectors are large and poorly aligned. In the noise-limited regime, high-quality data should be used as a signal amplifier: lowering the batch","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"must_read","source":"arxiv","sources":["arxiv"],"published_at":"2026-05-25T10:56:45+00:00","category_id":"scaling","cover_url":"/covers/2605_25698.png","hf_upvotes":0,"arxiv_categories":["cs.AI","cs.LG"],"summary":{"core_problem":"高质量数据（教材、数学证明、专家代码）是LLM训练中最稀缺的资源。当前业界普遍的做法是在训练后期引入高质量数据进行mid-training，但这与学习率衰减的训练动态存在根本冲突——模型在学习强度（每token有效更新幅度）最弱的时候才接触最好的数据。此前缺乏对数据质量调度与训练动力学联合优化的理论框架。","importance":"首次建立了数据质量感知的函数缩放定律（quality-aware functional scaling law），以解析形式给出了数据质量与batch size联合调度的最优解。提出的noise-limited/signal-limited双regime洞察是训练动力学理解上的概念性进展。DSR（Drop-Stable-Rampup）调度策略可直接应用于mid-training流水线，在15B MoE模型上验证了显著效果。","technical_approach":"将Li et al. (2026)的函数缩放定律扩展到时变数据质量维度，通过标签噪声方差的混合比例p(t)建模数据质量。超额风险（excess risk）分解为信号学习项和噪声积累项，两者均受数据质量调度p(t)和batch size调度b(t)的联合控制。将问题建模为变分优化问题，推导出渐近闭式最优调度（定理4.3）。解空间呈现两个regime：在noise-limited regime，高质量数据应作为信号放大器——降低batch size将更干净的数据转化为更多信号而不放大噪声；在signal-limited regime，高质量数据应作为噪声抑制器——后期放置减少终端噪声而不牺牲信号积累。基于理论提出DSR策略：在数据质量切换时降低batch size，保持低水平稳定以积累信号，最后ramp up抑制噪声。","experiments":"在15B MoE模型、108B tokens的mid-training设置下验证。DSR在14个基准上平均准确率相比WSD（Hu et al. 2024）提升+1.70，相比Cosine-decay提升+2.98。包含消融实验验证双regime理论的合理性，以及不同强度/内容/位置扰动的综合分析。","reading_guide":"第3节（质量感知函数缩放定律的推导）是理论核心。第4节（定理4.3及双regime分析）是关键概念洞察。第5节（DSR策略和实验结果）是实践落地部分。图2（regime可视化）和表1（主要结果）为必看图表。第3-4节的数学推导需要仔细阅读，附录可能包含完整证明。建议深度阅读：1.5-2小时。","authors_note":"未在提供内容中明确标注机构。理论深度和实验规模（15B MoE，108B tokens）表明可能来自大型AI研究机构。"},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"High-quality data (textbooks, mathematical proofs, expert-curated code) is the scarcest resource in LLM training. The current standard practice introduces high-quality data late for mid-training, but this fundamentally conflicts with learning rate decay dynamics—the model encounters its best data precisely when learning intensity (effective update magnitude per token) is weakest. No theoretical framework for the joint optimization of data quality scheduling and training dynamics previously existed.","importance":"Establishes the first quality-aware functional scaling law, providing closed-form optimal solutions for the joint scheduling of data quality and batch size. The two-regime insight (noise-limited vs signal-limited) represents a conceptual advance in understanding training dynamics. The DSR (Drop-Stable-Rampup) schedule is directly actionable for mid-training pipelines and has been validated at scale on a 15B MoE model.","technical_approach":"Extends functional scaling laws (Li et al. 2026) with a time-varying data quality dimension, modeling data quality through the mixing proportion p(t) of label noise variance. Excess risk decomposes into a signal-learning term and a noise-accumulation term, both jointly controlled by the data quality schedule p(t) and batch-size schedule b(t). Formulates as a variational optimization problem and derives asymptotic closed-form optimal schedules (Theorem 4.3). The solution exhibits two regimes: in the noise-limited regime, high-quality data acts as a signal amplifier—lowering batch size converts cleaner data into more signal without amplifying noise; in the signal-limited regime, it acts as a noise suppressor—late placement reduces terminal noise without sacrificing signal accumulation. Translates to DSR strategy: drop batch size at quality transition, hold stable low to accumulate signal, then ramp up to suppress noise.","experiments":"Validated on a 15B MoE model with 108B tokens in a mid-training setting. DSR improves average accuracy across 14 benchmarks by +1.70 over WSD (Hu et al. 2024) and +2.98 over Cosine-decay (Loshchilov and Hutter 2017). Includes ablation studies validating the two-regime theory and comprehensive analysis of corruptions with different intensity, content, and location.","reading_guide":"Section 3 (derivation of quality-aware functional scaling law) is the theoretical core. The key challenge is to maximize accuracy while minimizing the total number of generated tokens during reasoning. Recent PRM-guided methods score intermediate prefixes to steer this search, but most are frontier-only: they keep only the current active prefixes and irreversibly prune or resample away the rest using noisy PRM scores. This can cause premature commitment, div","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"skim","source":"arxiv","sources":["arxiv"],"published_at":"2026-05-24T15:48:57+00:00","category_id":"scaling","cover_url":"/covers/2605_25143.png","hf_upvotes":0,"arxiv_categories":["cs.AI","cs.LG"],"summary":{"core_problem":"这篇工作处理的是 test-time scaling 里一个很具体但常被忽略的问题：PRM 引导的搜索通常只保留当前 frontier，上一步被剪掉的 prefix 以后就再也回不来。若 PRM 对中间步骤打分有噪声，这种“只看前沿、不可回退”的机制会把局部误判放大成不可逆决策，导致过早承诺、搜索多样性塌缩，以及本来还能通向正确答案的轨迹被永久丢弃。论文的目标不是再训练一个更好的 PRM，而是在现有 PRM 不完美的前提下，重新设计搜索记忆与选择机制，让历史 prefix 仍可被重新访问，从而改善准确率与 token 开销之间的权衡。","importance":"对预训练本身没有直接贡献，但对 pretrain↔posttrain 接口很有参考价值，尤其是“模型能力固定时，如何把额外推理算力花得更有效”这个问题。它的重要性主要有两点。第一，论文把很多 TTS 方法的共同脆弱点讲清楚了：问题不只是 PRM 不准，而是 frontier-only search 把 PRM 的局部误差变成永久性剪枝。这个分析对理解 PRM、value model、search policy 的耦合关系很有帮助。第二，它提出的 persistent pool + stochastic backtracking，本质上是在有限内存下给搜索加入可恢复性，和训练时讨论 exploration、credit assignment、noisy proxy signal 时的思路是相通的。贡献量级更像是对现有 PRM-guided decoding 的稳健性改造，而不是新的推理范式。","technical_approach":"方法核心是把搜索状态从“当前 frontier”改成“历史 prefix 的持久池”。每一轮仍只扩展固定预算的 N 个新 prefix，因此运行 t 轮后池子大小至多是 Nt，内存是线性增长，而不是保存整棵指数级搜索树。关键点在于：历史 prefix 不会因为某一轮没被选中就彻底失效，后续仍可重新进入扩展候选。\n\n在这个框架上，论文给了两个具体实例。\n\n第一是 Subpool Selection。它不是直接在全历史池上做全局 top-k，因为那样仍会被少数被高估的错误 prefix 长期垄断；也不是均匀从历史里抽，因为那会把 token 浪费在明显陈旧或低质量状态上。它的做法是先从历史池随机抽一个子池，再在子池内部按 PRM 分数选 top 候选。这样做相当于在保留 PRM 引导的同时，给被“挡住”的低估 prefix 一个重新浮出的机会。可以把它理解成对贪心 PRM 排序加了一层受控随机化，目的是打破错误高分节点的长期阻塞。\n\n第二是 Power Backtrack SMC。论文把 SMC 风格的 sequential sampling 从 frontier 扩展到 persistent pool，允许从历史 prefix 混合采样，再用 powered PRM scores 和 mixture-corrected importance weights 做校正。这里的“power”相当于调节 PRM 分数对采样分布的尖锐程度；“mixture correction”则是因为候选来自多轮历史状态的混合分布，需要做重要性修正，避免估计偏掉。相比普通 SMC 只在当前粒子集上重采样，这个版本允许搜索在历史上回跳，因此更能从早期误剪枝中恢复。\n\n论文还花了不少篇幅说明理论对象与实际 PRM 的错位。理想目标是 prefix 的真实可完成性，即从该 prefix 出发最终得到正确答案的边缘概率；但现实中的 PRM 通常是用二值标签或外部 rollout 监督训练出来的代理分数，既不一定校准，也不一定匹配当前部署的 base LLM continuation behavior。作者强调，真正脆弱的不是“PRM 有噪声”这件事本身，而是 frontier-only 结构把这种噪声变成不可逆决策。这是全文最值得记住的机制判断。","experiments":"从全文可见，实验重点放在数学推理场景，比较的是 accuracy-token trade-off，而不是只看固定采样预算下的最终正确率。作者把方法和几类强基线比较：Best-of-N / self-consistency 这类独立完整采样方法，以及 beam / tree search / SMC 这类 PRM-guided frontier-only 方法。结论比较稳定：在相同 token 预算下，Subpool Selection 和 Power Backtrack SMC 往往能得到更高准确率；或者在达到相同准确率时，需要更少 token。\n\n论文强调这种收益在 PRM 分数有噪声、局部排序不可靠时更明显，因为历史可回访能修复早期误判。消融部分的主要信息有三类。第一，只做 persistent pool 但仍用过于贪心的全局选择，收益有限，说明“保留历史”本身不够，还需要合适的随机化选择机制。第二，随机化太强也不行，会退化成低效探索，因此子池大小、power 温度、重采样策略都在 exploitation 和 recovery 之间做平衡。第三，方法在不同模型尺度上都能改善 token 效率，说明它不是只对某个特定 base model 或 PRM 过拟合。\n\n给出的全文片段里没有完整表格数字，因此无法可靠复述每个 benchmark、参数量和提升幅度的精确值；但实验设计和结论方向是清楚的：作者不是追求绝对最高准确率，而是把“每个额外 token 是否花在更有恢复价值的轨迹上”作为核心指标。","reading_guide":"泛读时先看 Introduction 和 Problem Setup。这里把 frontier-only failure 讲得最清楚，尤其是理想 prefix target 与实际 PRM proxy 的区别。若只想抓住论文价值，这两部分已经够用。\n\n然后看两个方法部分：Subpool Selection 先读直觉和算法框图，再看它为什么能绕开高分错误节点的阻塞；Power Backtrack SMC 则重点看采样分布、powered score 和 mixture-corrected weights 的定义，不必一开始就陷进全部推导。\n\n实验建议优先看 accuracy-token 曲线，而不是单点结果。因为论文真正比较的是 token efficiency。再看 ablation：persistent pool、随机子池、power 参数、历史回访频率各自带来什么变化。预计 30–45 分钟可以完成一次有效泛读；若你做 PRM-guided search 或 test-time compute allocation，值得把方法细节和附录公式再过一遍。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"The paper targets a specific failure mode in PRM-guided test-time scaling: most search procedures are frontier-only. Once a prefix is pruned at an intermediate step, it is gone forever. If PRM scores on partial reasoning traces are noisy or misaligned with the deployed generator, this turns a local scoring error into an irreversible decision. The result is premature commitment, reduced diversity, and the loss of prefixes that could still lead to correct completions. The paper does not try to build a better PRM; it redesigns the search memory and selection mechanism so that previously generated prefixes remain revisitable.","importance":"This is not a pretraining paper, but it is relevant to the pretrain–posttrain interface because it studies how to spend extra inference compute more effectively when model weights are fixed. Its main value is conceptual. First, it isolates a structural weakness shared by many PRM-guided decoding methods: the issue is not only PRM noise, but the fact that frontier-only search makes local PRM mistakes permanent. Second, the proposed persistent-pool backtracking gives search a recovery mechanism under a linear-memory budget. That is useful for anyone thinking about noisy proxy signals, exploration under limited compute, or the coupling between reward models and search policies. The contribution is best read as a robustness improvement to existing PRM-guided reasoning, not as a new reasoning paradigm.","technical_approach":"$5","experiments":"$6","reading_guide":"$7","authors_note":""},"recommendation":{"zh":"Test-time scaling是LLM推理时计算分配的核心方向，随机回溯挑战了前沿搜索的不可逆假设，值得看方法部分和实验对比。","en":"Test-time scaling is central to LLM inference-time compute allocation; stochastic backtracking challenges the irreversible frontier search assumption—read the method section."},"metadata":{"institutions":[],"authors":["Dao Tran","Duc Anh Le","Ngoc Luu","Quan Pham","Tung Pham","Hung Bui"],"notable_authors":["Hung Bui"],"tags":["test_time_scaling","reasoning","PRM","search","compute_allocation"],"links":{"arxiv":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25143","pdf":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2605.25143","github":null,"project":null,"source_url":null,"article":null}}}]},{"id":"architecture","emoji":"🏗️","name":"LLM 架构与建模","description":"LLM 架构、MoE/SSM/混合架构、注意力结构、Diffusion/Flow/Masked/Any-order LM 等语言建模范式","papers":[{"id":"2605.25230","title":"Boosting Inference with Guided Reasoning: Stochastic Exploration for Recursive Models","abstract":"Recent work on recursive architectures has shown that tiny neural networks can be surprisingly powerful on structured reasoning tasks. compute allocation for recursive reasoning models: given a frozen TRM-style network, how can extra inference compute be used to explore multiple latent reasoning trajectories, and how can one tell in advance whether that exploration will help.","importance":"Its value for foundation-model research is not a new pretraining recipe, but a useful view of latent reasoning. Deterministic recursion is treated as approximate inference with a single particle and zero noise. The existing early-stopping Q-head is repurposed as a trajectory-level guide, rather than only a halting controller. This is relevant to compact recursive architectures, latent reasoning, and test-time compute allocation. The contribution is a principled inference-time mechanism with diagnostics, not a general LLM scaling result.","technical_approach":"The method, guided stochastic exploration, operates on a frozen recursive model. A TRM-style model repeatedly updates a latent state z and an answer state y with a shared backbone, and carries a learned Q-head trained to estimate whether the current decoded answer is correct. The paper injects stochastic perturbations into the inner recursion to propose a cloud of nearby latent trajectories. Candidate trajectories are then reweighted online using the Q-head through a Feynman–Kac-style tilt. The deterministic baseline is recovered as the one-particle, zero-noise limit. The key distinction from naive sampling is the diagnostic structure: exploration must be locally stable, and the guide must be aligned with success. The paper defines local stability, guide alignment, and cloud-token entropy as computable diagnostics for performance and reliability.","experiments":"The main empirical result is on structured reasoning. On Sudoku-Extreme, exact-solve accuracy improves from 85.9±0.6% to 98.0±0.3% without retraining, and the method recovers strong performance at lower recursion depths. The particle cloud contains many more successful trajectories than the deterministic path, and the Q-head is sufficiently aligned to select them, closing most of the oracle-particle gap. Maze-Hard serves as a failure case. Exploration is stable, but the Q-head scores are too flat; a label-free in-cloud guide-spread bound flags this before using labels, and evaluation confirms the lack of improvement. Entropy statistics over final-step and path-integrated token marginals rank token-level error risk and support selective abstention on both the standard test split and a harder deterministic-failure split.","reading_guide":"A useful skim takes about 25–35 minutes. Prior Large Concept Models mostly focus on English, even though their embedding-decoder interface can in principle support multilingual generation. Mimir asks whether large-scale multilingual pretraining over concept sequences can improve coverage of non-English semantic distributions.","importance":"The main relevance for pretraining research is the modeling unit. This makes the work useful for thinking about semantic granularity, multilingual representation sharing, and the new bottlenecks introduced by encoder-decoder concept interfaces. To handle this, we study a sleep-like consolidation mechanism in which a model periodically converts recent context into persistent fast weights before clearing its key-value cache. During sleep, the model performs N offline recurrent passes over the accumulated context and updates the fast weights in its state-space model (SSM) blocks throug","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"skim","source":"huggingface","sources":["huggingface"],"published_at":"2026-05-25T00:00:00.000Z","category_id":"architecture","cover_url":"/covers/2605_26099.png","hf_upvotes":10,"arxiv_categories":[],"summary":{"core_problem":"在“有限 KV cache + 固定大小快权重（fast weights）”的混合注意力/SSM 架构里，长上下文问题不只来自存储容量，还来自“被逐出上下文窗口的 token 需要多少计算才能被压缩成可用于后续推理的内部状态”。论文要解决的是：当问题需要更深的推理链路（reasoning depth）时，传统一次前向的快权重写入不足以把已见上下文转化为可用的长期状态，导致即便快权重容量足够，模型在窗口清空后仍无法正确推理。","importance":"对预训练研究的直接启发不在于数据/优化器，而在于“计算-记忆”接口：把额外计算从逐 token 的 wake-time 预测阶段，转移到离线的 consolidation 阶段，从而在不增加在线延迟的前提下提升跨窗口推理能力。它把“循环/迭代计算”引入到记忆写入过程，而不是推理输出过程，提供了一种不同于长上下文注意力、RAG、或单步快权重更新的路径。对做混合架构（attention+SSM/fast weights）和长程推理的团队，价值在于：给出了一个可控的诊断结论（深推理时 GDN 失败并非容量不足）以及一个可端到端训练的机制（sleep recurrence）来补足写入计算。","technical_approach":"核心设计是“sleep-like consolidation”。模型在推理时维持一个有限上下文窗口（KV cache）用于高保真近期访问，同时在 SSM/快权重块里维护固定大小的权重态（如 GDN/Mamba2 风格的外积/Delta-rule 更新）。当窗口满了，模型进入 sleep：\n1) 冻结外部输入（不再接收新 token），对累积的上下文做 N 次离线循环前向（offline recurrent passes）。\n2) 在每次 pass 中，通过一个学习到的局部更新规则（learned local rule）递归更新快权重状态，使其逐步吸收上下文信息。这里的关键不是换一种快权重公式，而是允许“多步迭代写入”，把写入过程变成一个可学习的计算过程。\n3) consolidation 完成后清空 KV cache，回到 wake-time：后续 token 预测保持单次前向、低延迟；跨窗口信息主要依赖已被写入的快权重。\n训练上，作者强调端到端反传穿过 sleep 过程，直接优化“sleep 之后的任务表现”。这点把 sleep 视作模型计算图的一部分，而不是推理时的启发式缓存压缩。\n与已有 looped/depth-recurrent 模型的区别：以往循环多用于提升每步预测的计算量（loop at prediction time）；这里循环主要用于提升记忆写入/巩固的计算量（loop at consolidation time），从而把额外 compute
